# Global Real Estate Strategy When will (listed) real estate rebound?

## A sharp fall in valuations has also created opportunities

The sector fell 5% in February in the face of rate volatility and CRE bank exposure. This means the sector is down 5% since the start of the year, missing the market's rally, resulting in 10pp underperformance. Negative newsflow in the direct market is more a lagging than a leading indicator for the indirect real estate market, in our view. For one element, the issues in CRE debt do not appear systemic, as our Global Strategy team recently assessed; and while office is seeing rising delinquencies, other sectors have proven resilient (Figure 2); second, listed real estate typically anticipates (and usually overstates, due to leverage) the underlying real estate cycles, a study from EPRA highlighting how the listed sector returns lead those in the direct market by two to four quarters in most assessed markets; and (3) even at the direct market, there are green shoots. Our colleagues from UBS Asset Management think "the bulk of the correction had occurred by the end of 2023"; and Jon Gray, President of the Blackstone Group, mentioned believing "that real estate values are bottoming" and that the sharp slowdown in new construction starts in commercial real estate "is quite positive for long-term values".

## YTD review – Hotels top sector

Hotels (+3.3%) and Specialty (-0.9%) were the best performers. Office (-8.8%) and Diversified (-6.6%) performed the worst. By region, JP Devs (+12.2%) and Australia (- 0.1%) outperformed. REITs (-4.2%) outperformed Non-REITs (-5.9%).

## Global Valuation (excluding emerging markets)

We estimate \~10% return potential for our coverage universe (Feb-end) ex-EM, standing at an 13.0% discount to our NAV estimate, 2024E P/E at 18.4x, 2024E DPS yield at 4.0%.

## Global Top Picks

CapitaLand Ascendas, Frasers Centrepoint Trust, Mapletree Industrial Trust, CapitaLand Investment (SG); Sino Land, Henderson, Link REIT, ESR (HK); COLI, CR Land, KE Holdings, CMSK (China); GLP J-REIT, Nippon Prologis, Mitsubishi Estate (JP); GPT, Dexus (AU); TECOM Group, Shurgard, Merlin, CTP (Cont. Europe); British Land, Derwent (UK); Sun Communities, Prologis, Equity Residential, Equinix, American Tower, SBA Comm (US).

# Listed Global Real Estate: still affected by macro, rather than fundamentals

CRE delinquency rates by property type continue to show a bifurcated picture: office rising, but multifamily, retail, lodging and industrial falling.

Valuation. Globally, the PNAV is at a 13% discount, at 0.3SD cheaper than its long-term historical level. The issue we are now facing is that the "Asset Value" in NAV is under downward pressure as compressed cap rates need to expand. Valuation on an implied cap rate basis is 0.6SD lower ('more expensive') than the full 30-year history. Numerically, the level is 5.6% EBITDA/EV, vs. the long-term average of 6.4%.

## Hot or not? Regional relative value

We have updated our global 'heat maps' database, which looks at regional valuations across four key valuation metrics (previously we looked at six, but have dropped PCF and PBV as these are approximated by PE and PNAV respectively - please contact us if you would like to see this data). The following 'heat map' presents the output. Across valuation metrics, we show the current pricing of each country relative to its own history. Darker red indicates more expensive, and we have "capped" the shading at 2 standard deviations above average. Darker blue is cheaper, again capped at 2 standard deviations. Lighter shades are more in line with the long-term average. Within each chart, we give the full \~30-year history for each metric, with the size of the bar indicating the most recent data point.

Globally, now the listed real estate sector is trading below the historical average on P/ NAV basis, but more expensive relative to history on EBITDA/EV, as discussed above.

Global Credit Strategy - Commercial real estate and shadow banking: what we worry about... and what we don't (Report)

## Commercial real estate and shadow banking outlook: 4 key takeaways

In terms of risks to the outlook, US commercial real estate and shadow banking are two of the ‘hotter’ topics in discussions with market participants. Our top-down analysis supports several key conclusions: 1) US commercial real estate debt losses do not pose a financial stability risk; 2) those US banks most impacted by CRE exposure have a very small footprint in the broader credit ecosystem; we prefer US IG bank bonds to REITs; 3) the exposure of European banks overall to US CRE is modest and recent volatility should normalize; we remain bullish EU bank bonds; and 4) shadow banking in the US and Europe merits monitoring, but an improving outlook for corporate and bank credit in both regions should allay investor concerns.

## We do not think US CRE credit losses pose a financial stability risk

US CRE property values were estimated at \~\$20tn in late 2023, backing CRE debt of \~ \$5tn; for comparison, EU CRE figures are about 55%/40% lower at EUR \~9tn and 3tn, respectively. The largest holders of CRE equity, namely open and closed-end funds, are most at-risk. But overall US (and EU) CRE debt is still a smaller share of GDP at \~20% (\~15%). For comparison, US C&I and mortgage debt are \~50-55% of GDP. The largest US sub- sector is multifamily at \~\$2tn, of which half is backed by agency/ GSE pools and implicitly lower credit risk. The US office sector is under the most pressure from higher delinquencies due to rising vacancy rates and falling property values. For US, office property values are estimated at \~3tn, backed by about \~0.75tn in debt. For context, a 50% decline in office property values, or \$1.5tn unlevered, is less than the \~\$2tn rise in the value of the S&P 500 in Feb month. Focusing on office (and downtown retail) debt of \~1.1tn, about two-thirds (0.7tn) is held by banks, of which 71% (0.5tn) is on the smallest bank balance sheets (below \$100bn in assets).

## Banks with high CRE exposure have a small footprint in credit supply

We have consistently downplayed the importance of US regionals in supplying credit Our updated stock charts show non-banks and the largest banks (>250bn) hold 90% of C&I, 85% of mortgage, 75% of consumer and 60% of outstanding loans/debt (“the stock”). Over the latest one-year period, the largest banks in essence originated 100% of C&I, 100% of mortgage, 63% of consumer and 57% of CRE loans provided by the banks ("the flow"). But this paints all smaller banks with a broad brush; individual bank exposures are more nuanced, and post the latest regional bank volatility, what regulators are looking at is an increasingly important consideration. For more granularity we used the 2007 regulatory guidance, recently updated, to isolate banks that could face more scrutiny over CRE exposures (specifically those with CLD loans > 100% of total capital, or those with CRE > 300% of capital and >50% growth over 3yrs).

We show the cohort of banks that meet either criteria in terms of share of bank loans and bank and non-bank loans/ debt. On the latter, this cohort holds about \$1.5tn in assets and \$1.1tn in net loans (\~3%); by major sector they account for 1-2% of C&I and mortgage debt, less than 1% of consumer, and 10-25% of CRE loans (dependent on sub-sector). A different approach to estimating banks at-risk based on insolvency tests, due to CRE stress and market value declines from higher rates, implies banks with \$1- 1.4tn in assets could be at-risk. But note we view these estimates as the upper end, arguably playing out over time, and not incorporating reactive monetary policy action.

## European banks' exposure to US CRE is modest and manageable

According to our European bank equity analysts, CRE loan exposure at European banks is approximately EUR 1.4tn, or 7% of total loans. An ECB supervisory letter suggests about EUR 410bn, or 29%, is foreign CRE exposure. Foreign (and domestic) office exposure specifically is about EUR 120bn (and EUR 130bn), or 8% (9%) of the total. In terms of country exposure, among the Top 10 by size, banks in Austria (11%), Germany (10%) and Netherlands (9%) have higher CRE concentrations as a share of total loans; banks in Denmark (4%), Spain (4%) and France (5%) have lower exposures. Overall, we believe recent elevated volatility in EU banks related to CRE concerns among smaller German banks should normalize over the medium term as we go through the ECB’s cutting cycle; we reiterate our bullish view on EU financials.

## A solid corporate outlook should ease shadow banking risks

Shadow banking, or in this context, bank loans to non-bank financials (NBFIs), has grown at a much faster rate than traditional lending post-GFC. In the US, bank credit commitments to NBFIs is approaching \$2tn, with roughly 50% drawn. But we believe a lot of this risk is heavily codependent on the outlook for corporate credit risk. First, US C&I/corporate credit is the largest by far in terms of the size of debt outstanding originated by non-banks and not implicitly government guaranteed. Second, a breakdown of bank commitments to NBFIs highlights a strong dependence on corporate-related exposures; 20% is to other vehicles (including pension, closed-end, investment, and hedge funds), 18% is to real estate credit, mortgage brokers and lessors, and 17% is to special purpose entities, CLOs and ABS. All three have seen above average growth since 2018, but SPEs/CLOs/ABS growth has been strongest. Third, in Europe, similarly about two-thirds of the \~EUR 21tn in private sector debt outstanding is to non-financial corporates (NFCs); and, despite a more bank-centric lending model, non-banks account for about 60% of credit to NFCs (i.e., \~60% of GDP). In that context, our more constructive corporate debt outlook given robust US credit conditions, easing stress in riskier credit sectors, and strong EU credit technicals should placate some of the flashpoints for shadow banking risks.

## February A-REITs - GMG powers strong Industrial returns

S&P/ASX Industrial A-REITs returned +16.3%, S&P/ASX Retail A-REITs returned +1.1%, S&P/ASX Diversified A-REITs returned -1.6% and S&P/ASX Office A-REITs returned - 5.6%. Outperformers were GMG (+16.8%), HMC (+14.8%), and INA (+9.2%). Underperformers were GPT (-6.7%), CNI (-6.4%), and DXS (-5.6%). For the past 12 mths rolling, the top-performing AREITs have been HMC (+66.4%) and GMG (+52.6%) while the worst performers are CLW (-13.0%) and CQE (-12.0%). (Link)

## 1H24 results in review

The S&P/ASX 200 Property Accumulation index returned +5.1% in February, outperforming the S&P/ ASX 200, which returned +0.8%. GMG powers strong Industrial returns. Included in the FTSE EPRA NAREIT, announced 29-Feb-23.

We revised FY24/25 earnings +1%/+1% on average through 1H24 reporting season. Our largest FY24e revisions were made for HMC (+21%, HMCCP) and LLC (-19%, lower dev't contribution). Key themes from results include: 1) capital position: BS distress looks less likely given asset sales are progressing and the rates outlook has improved; 2) guidance: while there were beats and misses relative to our forecasts, guidance was generally held, although investors will focus on 2H skews; and 3) FY25 growth: UBSe FY25 growth has increased slightly (to 5.4% from 5.1%) with further upside should 2HCY24 rate cuts eventuate. By subsector: Resi: better trading than feared with some green shoots; Office: generally positive leasing outcomes for the right assets; Logistics: robust releasing spreads which should moderate albeit strong LFL growth still expected; Retail: solid operating metrics, cost headwinds subsiding for mid-cap names, capex a focus for large-cap names; Storage: relatively stable operating conditions; Funds Managers: subdued conditions but likely closer to a recovery. (Link)

GMG (+16.8%) - GMG's 1H24 result was +13% ahead and guidance was upgraded from 9% growth to 11%. Medium-term earnings growth is now underpinned by data centre development which the market has quickly priced in. Transactional development profits are now >\$1.1bn pa (\~50% of EBIT) and are reliant on core capital yet to be raised. While risks are increased, GMG's premium assets, secured land/power bank, proven expertise, tenant demand (incl. AI) and strength of capital partners mitigates this, although we downgraded to Neutral given a more balanced risk/reward profile. Included in the FTSE EPRA NAREIT, announced 29-Feb-23.

HMC (+14.8%) - HMC's 1H24 FFO (16.6c, +100% vs pcp) was 41% above UBSe and we see guidance of \~32c (pre-tax, UBSe \~36c) as very achievable. The focus is now on HMC's significant growth aspirations (\$20b AUM vs \$8.5b currently) and plans to transition to a global diversified alternative asset manager. HMC have provided some guidance on the path to \$20bn with organic growth expected from new strategies (energy transition, global health, digital infra).

INA (+9.2%) - INA's 1H24 result saw underlying profit of \$43.5m +19%/24% vs. UBSe/ cons. \$36.5m/\$35.2m and Underlying EPS of 10.7c vs. UBSe/cons. 9.0c/8.6c. The beat was +5% at the EBIT line and was driven by better rental income (helped by CPI+ rent reviews and dev't completions) and Holidays. Following the 1H24 result and a period of strong performance, we downgraded INA to Neutral from Buy, noting FY24e guidance was unchanged which implies a weaker 2H24.

Following the 1H24 result and a period of strong performance, we downgraded INA to Neutral from Buy, noting FY24e guidance was unchanged which implies a weaker 2H24.

## New Zealand REITs - NZ REITs Quarterly Update

NZ REITs within our coverage outperformed the NZX50 by +2.8% during 4Q23. NZ REITs within our coverage returned +6.9% during the last quarter of 2023, followed by -0.3% performance during Jan-24. This compares to +4.2% and +0.9% for the NZX50, for the respective periods. The sector outperformance during 4Q23 was mainly driven by a lower NZ 10Y bond yields (down \~95bps during 4Q23 to 4.4%). However, YTD bond yields lifted again by \~30bps. The latest JLL data suggests the Auckland Prime Office subsector saw the greatest YoY uplift in net effective rents, and on a quarterly basis the Auckland Prime CBD Retail sector saw the biggest uplift (+4%). We recently analysed local KiwiSaver funds' positioning moving into 2024, which suggests KiwiSaver funds are most overweight GMT relative to NZX50 market weights (+0.9%). On average,

KiwiSaver funds are overweight NZ REITs by +0.6% relative to the NZX50, vs +0.5% 12- months ago. We maintain our Neutral view on the sector as we expect cap rates to soften further during CY24. PCT is our most preferred NZ REIT, due to: 1) its high-quality, well-located Auckland office assets; and 2) attractive valuation vs. history. (Link)

Singapore real estate returned -3.7% in Feb-2024, underperforming Global by 3.3pp.

## Budget 2024: All about AVs & ABSD

Budget 2024 was presented on 16 February. Its theme—"Building our shared future together"—alludes to the focus on long-term strategic priorities and fortifying the social compact. For property, the changes in annual value (AV) bands and Additional Buyer's Stamp Duty (ABSD) regime is a small net positive though we see limited incremental upside for property developers. We continue to like SREITs over Developers. (Link)

## Crowding scores: The long and short of it

Positioning in SREITs improved in Dec/Jan but likely reversed into Feb over dashed hopes of a March Fed rate cut. The sector remains short crowded and we see heightened trading volatility, particularly around Fed speak. That said, as views on the timing and magnitude of potential rate cuts converge, we expect crowded shorts to narrow and investors currently sidelined to increase weights. Under-owned bellwether SREITs like Mapletree Industrial Trust (MINT) could benefit from capital inflows. For crowded shorts that revolve around risk on themes (exposure to high gearing, China, Europe), there could also be bottom fishing activity. We continue to favour SREITs over Developers and those with defensive characteristics like CapitaLand Ascendas REIT (CLAR), Frasers Centrepoint Trust (FCT) and MINT. (Link)

We continue to favour SREITs over Developers and those with defensive characteristics like CapitaLand Ascendas REIT (CLAR), Frasers Centrepoint Trust (FCT) and MINT.

## CapitaLand Investment - Bold targets imply impetus to divest and diversify

Results came in below expectations though key drivers have been well flagged. 2023 Operating PATMI of S\$568m (-7% YoY) was reported, forming 78% of our estimates. S \$600m of China-driven FV declines drove a steep -79% YoY decline in headline PATMI to S\$181m. A new S\$200bn FUM target in 5 years was announced, with continued capital recycling and platform M&A as next steps. On the former, the goal is to dilute the existing exposure to China through divestments, with increased investments in India and the developed markets. In so doing, double-digit headline ROEs are achievable, though this could take some time vis-à-vis core PATMI of 3.9% achieved in 2023. A cash dividend of 12¢ was announced, stable YoY. We note that CLI's share price has corrected 12% since its profit warning in Dec '23, suggesting that the negatives are partly in the price. (Link)

## City Developments - Pursuing ≥S\$1bn of divestments in 2024

CDL reported 2023 adj. PATMI of S\$189m which was below consensus/UBS full-year estimate of S\$342m/S\$374m. Higher-than-expected finance costs was the key drag. An 8¢ final dividend was proposed (2023: 12¢, 2022: 28¢). Weaker performance aside, management sounded positive on divestments in 2024. We think hotel divestments could gather pace alongside ongoing hospitality tailwinds and this could unlock meaningful gains for CDL (given historical cost accounting). Furthermore, management also alluded to the prospect of ensuing special dividends. (Link)

## Japan Equity Strategy - Tailwind for Japanese stocks; expectations for structural changes to spread gradually

After increasing +25% in 2023, TOPIX has risen a further 13% in the first two months of 2024. In addition to growing expectations for semiconductor-related stocks related to generative AI, firmness of the US economy, continued dollar strength against the yen, and relative positioning within Asia compared with China, expectations for reform by Japanese companies are emerging (Link). In addition to increasing share buybacks since last year as a step to prevent PBR from dipping below 1X, the unwinding of strategic shareholdings is another topic of strong interest. We are focusing on changes in investment strategies and shareholder return policy, not as temporary measures but alongside progress in 'Cost of Capital management'. Specifically, we see a possibility of revisions to business portfolios including M&A. (Link)

We recommend JREITs, as real estate developers have outperformed in the last three months to JREIT, TOPIX, and global peers, and the share price discount to NAV has shrunk significantly.

Most favoured - GLP J-REIT, Nippon Prologis REIT, Mitsubishi Estate. Least favoured - Japan Real Estate.

We recommend JREITs, as real estate developers have outperformed in the last three months to JREIT, TOPIX, and global peers, and the share price discount to NAV has shrunk significantly.

## Sumitomo Realty & Development - Solid earnings from office/hotel/housing priced in, downgraded to Neutral

Given the recent improvement in office vacancy rates, strong hotel rates and home sales, we have revised our earnings forecast and price target for Sumitomo R&D, based on the prospects for ROE to remain above 9%. However, these positive elements are already mostly priced in, in our view. The discount to post-tax NAV reported by the company has narrowed from roughly 65% as of the full-year results announcement last May to around 50% today. The valuation is now on a par with sector peer Mitsubishi Estate. Similarly, the discount to NAV based on our estimate has shrunk from 55% to 40%. The P/B has also improved, from 0.8X to 1.2X. We therefore downgraded our rating to Neutral from Buy. If there were continued improvements in the office market and in hotel rates and sustainable sales price hikes sufficient for houses to absorb cost inflation, these could constitute reasons for a more positive outlook. (Link)

## Mitsui Fudosan - Upcoming asset turnover likely factored in

We rated Mitsui Fudosan shares Buy until now assuming it would accelerate sales of commercial real estate assets such as offices and Oriental Land shares and that share buybacks would rise to ¥60bn from ¥30bn a year (45% total return ratio eventually rising to about 60%). Since the May 2023 results, the share price discount to after-tax NAV (net assets including unrealised gains on real estate) disclosed by the company has improved from 50% to about 30% now, pulling away from the 50% levels at peers Mitsubishi Estate and Sumitomo Realty. Similarly, the value we calculate has improved from 60% to 40%, and PBR has improved to 1.2X, from 0.8X. Good commercial real estate sales prices and strong office and hotel businesses have gradually been recognised, and on 5 February, a ¥1trn share buyback and sale of Oriental Land shares worth ¥0.5trn proposed by Elliott Management in the US suggested future asset turnover has been factored into the share price all at once. We therefore downgraded to Neutral from Buy. If there were an environment conducive to resets of the initial rate of asset turnover and profit growth, these could be grounds for a more positive view. (Link)

Hong Kong real estate returned +4.7% in Feb-2024, outperforming Global by 5.0pp.

## Hong Kong Property - How long is property sales recovery?

After the complete residential stamp duties removal on 28 Feb, new home sales and secondary transactions saw a strong rally. However, we think a sustained recovery will require: i) China economic growth recovery; ii) US Fed rate cut; and/or iii) Hong Kong retaining its uniqueness over other tier 1 cities in mainland China. We still maintain our forecast of residential prices to decline by \~5% in 2024E (better than previous forecast of 5-10% decline), due to: 1) previous case studies from tier 1 cities' relaxations; 2) potential supply from higher secondary listings due to Special Stamp Duty (SSD) relaxations; 3) primary inventories remaining high; 4) UBS Evidence Lab survey on mainland buyers' intentions and reasons for purchasing HK properties; and 5) our channel check from Shenzhen and Shanghai Centaline regarding onshore purchase intentions. (Link)

## Hong Kong Property - 2024-25 budget positive for developers

HK government announced 2024-25 Budget Speech, which carried some positive surprises to the market. The biggest surprise, albeit in line with our expectation, is the complete removal of excess stamp duties on residential property transactions and incremental liquidity measures to developers. We believe that may support the liquidity for highly leveraged developers, hence partly relieve new home sales pricing pressure. As a result, we revised up our residential price forecast to down \~5%, from previous 5-10% decline. However, the sustainability will depend on upcoming sell through for new project launches & weekly secondary transaction volume. We see residential market sentiment improving in 2H24, if residential rents further recover and mortgage costs decline. For commercial real estate, we think the Budget Speech is a positive for retail & REIT sector, but negative for hotel sector. Key picks - Sino Land, Henderson, Link REIT. (Link)

## China Property - February 2024 contract sales weakened as expected

CRIC reported top 100 developers' contract sales for February 2024. Top 100 developers' contract sales declined by 62% YoY, vs -36% YoY in January 2023, and declined by 22% MoM. The YoY decline widened in February 2024 due to Chinese New Year effect and reduced project launches, as CREIS 12-city newly launched units dropped 68% MoM. Combining Jan and Feb 2024, 2M2024 top 100 developers contract sales declined by 51% YoY, vs -37% YoY decline in December 2023, vs our previous expectation of -50% YoY (see note). Looking ahead, we expect top 100 developers' contract sales YoY to recover after April 2024, as the base effect peaked in April 2023. (Link)

## China Property Management - 2023 results preview; Prefer CR Mixc and Poly PM

After the complete residential stamp duties removal on 28 Feb, new home sales and secondary transactions saw a strong rally. However, we think a sustained recovery will require: i) China economic growth recovery; ii) US Fed rate cut; and/or iii) Hong Kong retaining its uniqueness over other tier 1 cities in mainland China.

We expect major property managers' 2023 core earnings growth to be +8% YoY compared with +25%/19% YoY in 1H23/2022 . As per our talks with property managers, top property managers saw more intense competition in third-party projects bidding in both existing residential properties and non-residential properties due to shrinking primary residential market in 2023. Among managers, we expect CR Mixc 2023 earnings growth is +29% YoY; COPH earnings growth is +25% YoY; Poly PM and Onewo earnings growth have a +20% YoY increase; and CG Services earnings growth declines 90% YoY due to goodwill and receivable impairment in 2023. We think SOEbacked property managers will outperform the sector with 2023 core earnings growth of over 20% YoY, as they have less negative impact from their associated developers. Our top picks are CR Mixc on shopping malls C-REITs rollout and Poly Property Services (Poly PM) on potential dividend payout increase, following their parent company’s recently announced dividend payout increase. Given the high net cash as % of market cap (at 40%) and high cash flow yield (18% OCF yield on ex-cash market cap basis), any dividend payout change will be crucial to share price. (Link)

2024-25 budget positive for developers. We revise up HK residential price forecast from 5-10% decline to down \~ 5% in 2024E. Key picks - Sino Land, Henderson, Link REIT.

Looking ahead, we expect top 100 developers' contract sales YoY to recover after April 2024, as the base effect peaked in April 2023.

SOE managers continue to outperform with over 20% YoY earnings growth. Prefer CR Mixc and Poly PM .

## China Property - Marketing feedback: Investors Stay Defensive

## Investors turns more defensive and bearish

We met with over 80 investors in mainland China, Hong Kong and Singapore in January 2024 for our latest sector upgrade report on the recently announced urban village renovation and social housing initiatives. Overall, investors are skeptical about the programmes' effectiveness due to a lack of details on, eg, size, timetable, % of monetary compensation, how to balance interest of stakeholders. Investors also appear to think the sector upgrade is too early, as the physical market is deteriorating (see note on January 2024 sales), whilst demand contribution from urban village renovation has not kicked in. Hence, investors have turned more bearish and defensive, and reduced their positioning to extremely underweight or zero weighting. However, the level of interest in the sector remains high given the implications for macro and construction activities. (Link)

We met with over 80 investors in mainland China, Hong Kong and Singapore in January 2024 for our latest sector upgrade report on the recently announced urban village renovation and social housing initiatives.

## Different interests of investors

Hong Kong investors appear interested in long and short stock ideas, mainly on the upcoming 2023 earnings and balance sheet risk and hence are interested in cash flow analysis. Mainland investors are more bearish than offshore investors, due to disappointment with the policy easing measures in the past. They are concerned that property prices in tier 1 cities may see further downside risk unless the central government can take further action. Singapore investors have the lowest weighting in property stocks as they are more flexible about investing elsewhere. They are more interested in the macro implications of the sector down-cycle, eg suspended projects, property price declines. (Link)

## Data points to monitor

We think tier 1 cities' property price stabilization is most important, and an early indicator of that is secondary listing. 50-city secondary listing seems to have stabilized after a 30% increase in 2023, whilst the four tier 1 cities secondary listings have declined by 2.6% since the peak in November 2023, and declined by 1.2% YTD. In addition, PBoC's PSL funding data is crucial to support village renovation. In Dec 2023 and Jan 2024 combined, PSL funding increased by Rmb500bn. Separately, 19 cities announced they have obtained Rmb865bn credit facility from the two policy banks on urban village renovation. (Link)

Three takeaways for March - (1) Short interest for European real estate remained at February level, as risk of delayed rate cuts continue to hover around (slide 3); (2) Within European real estate, Covivio, SBB, and Balder saw rising short interest, while Fabege, British Land, and Big Yellow Group saw the reverse (slide 5); and (3) Three of the top five most shorted stocks are SBB, Balder, and Aroundtown (slide 5). ## UK Real Estate - A counter cyclical opportunity or catching a falling knife?

We have made rating changes in UK REITs, having taken a deep dive into the Central London REITs focusing on the development pipelines and forward looking capital growth estimates. We analysed the pipelines line-by-line utilizing CBRE's prime submarket yield data and active market participants to provide an independent view on the companies assumptions. To analyse differences in the standing portfolios we developed bespoke benchmarks for each company based on the MSCI index and used this tool to create portfolio level capital growth assumptions. We think this is an opportunity to buy into high quality Central London office portfolios at a point in time that the market is overplaying the downside risks from structural changes. If our thesis is proved correct we see the most upside in British Land & Derwent, both with current pipelines which are positioned in submarkets that we see having very strong rental dynamics. We are more cautious on Landsec due to their development pipeline focus on the South Bank submarket and longer term regional urban redevelopments, and GPE due to the forecast for a sharp drop in earnings in 2024. (Link)

We have made rating changes in UK REITs, having taken a deep dive into the Central London REITs focusing on the development pipelines and forward looking capital growth estimates. Most favoured- British Land & Derwent London.

## URW - Increased PT and staying Neutral as investment market re-opening key to further deleveraging

We published our updated model of URW, increased our price target by 47% to €73 and keeping our Neutral rating unchanged. The main driver to our higher price target is our 25-35% higher EPRA NTA estimates over the forecast period, due to our revised view that disposals will be completed at a smaller discount than previously assumed, supported with improved operations in the US shopping centre market, where URW has disposals plans. Our EPS estimates increased by 2% to 15%, driven by higher rental growth and a more progressive disposal process, as the above improved fundamentals reduce the pressure to exit the US in the short term. (Link)

## Swedish CRE - Financing cost better contained in Q4 but challenges remain

With all of the top 30 Swedish CRE companies having now reported FY results, we update our CRE database. Q4 has shown a weaker trend in operating results than prior quarters (+9% y/y but -9% q/q). Seasonal weakness is normal, but we also note an underlying slowdown with y/y growth \~1.5 percentage points lower than in Q3. This may partly be explained by asset disposals, weaker net lettings and lower rental income growth. In line with trends seen in 2022, Q4 has seen a larger than average revaluation of properties (-2.0% on average), bringing the cumulative revaluation in our sample to \~10% in 2023. At the end of last year we saw a number of companies take advantage of lower interest rates and somewhat surprising, average funding costs were stable q/q. We still see some headwinds from cheap, maturing debt, but could the worst be behind us? (Link)

## Merlin Properties - FY23: Portfolio values hold firm, data centre expansion accelerates

Merlin delivered solid operations as expected, but stronger-than-expected portfolio valuation. LFL rental growth averaged 6.5%, but FFO fell 2.1% to €284.2m (UBSe: €280.1m), or €0.61 p.s., in line with consensus (UBSe: €0.60). (Link)

## Shurgard Self-Storage - FY 23: Slight moderation in growth, but medium-term guidance reiterated

Key metrics were in-line, the 2024 may look prudent but medium-term guidance is reiterated. Property operating revenue increased by 9% to €357.7m, slightly below consensus at €359.5m and UBSe at €360.6m. Same store property operating revenue grew by 5.7% (FY 22: 8.6%). Same store NOI margin was 67.6%, improving by 0.9% from FY 2022, driven by economies of scale. Shurgard has provided its FY 2024 guidance, expecting a c.7.5% revenue growth for FY 2024 (UBSe: +10%). Shurgard is one of our top picks given recent share underperformance and robust long-term tailwinds. (Link)

The shares trade at a 41% discount to reported EPRA NTA and 38% below UBSe trough EPRA NTA of €14.37, which makes shares attractive in our view.

## Aedifica - FY23: results and outlook both in line

Aedifica reported FY 23 results and 2024 outlook both in-line with consensus. Rental income was +15% y-o-y to €314.2m (UBSe: €312.9m) driven by completed developments plus rent indexation, which amounts to +5.2% like-for-like rental growth. EPRA earnings were up 21% y-o-y to €219.6m (UBSe: €218.1m). Dividend is proposed up 3% to €3.80 p.s. (UBSe: €3.80), consistent with guidance. (Link)

We have a Buy on Aedifica as we expect re-rating following its improved balance sheet and robust fundamentals.

## PSP Swiss Property - Strong operational execution - reiterate Buy

Following recent FY23 results, we roll forward our model and slightly lower our adjusted EPS estimates by 0.6% on average for FY24-26. We slightly reduced our PT to CHF128 (130), on the back of reduced revaluation gain assumptions. We continue to like PSP due to its: 1) solid balance sheet (FY23 net LTV of 35%), 2) hard to replicate prime portfolio with limited retail exposure (15% of rental income), and 3) predictable capital allocation strategy from a well-regarded management team. (Link)

High visibility on further dividend hikes. Reiterate Buy.

## U.S. REITs - Are Investors Looking Past 2024 With a Focus on 2025?

Now is an opportune time to dive into REIT 2024 outlooks, forecasts for 2024 & 2025, earnings growth algorithms, and valuations. The timing is appropriate as 4Q'23 earnings season has concluded, REITs have provided guidance for the upcoming year, consensus models should be updated, and we are now more than two-thirds of the way through 1Q'24. Our stock selection philosophy remains centered on REIT subsectors and individual stocks that offer high visibility into sustainable or accelerating earnings algorithms. Our deep dive has several conclusions.

We use several regression analyses to highlight that current REIT FFO valuations are surprisingly not driven by 2024 estimates, but rather by 2025 forecasts.

 Muted growth is expected for 2024: The consensus forecast for 2024 FFO growth for the REIT industry is 3.1%, on average. Notably, this suggests a material slowdown from the 2023 average growth of 3.9%. We think that factors influencing the deceleration include a moderating macroeconomic environment, headwinds from higher interest rates on financing costs, and limited contributions from external growth.

REIT valuations vs. the U.S. 10-yr remain elevated: The current spread between REIT industry dividend yields and the U.S. 10-yr is 90 bps, down from the start of the year of 100 bps. So far in 2024, REITs have seen negative total returns, but the 10-yr has also increased. To put this in context, the spread over the longterm has averaged 200 bps. We think the narrow spread and limited visibility into lower 10-yr yields could make it difficult for generalist investors to rotate into REITs, potentially keeping incremental buyers on the sidelines. Applying the UBS Economics forecast for a year-end 2024 US 10-yr forecast of 3.6% suggests that the spread would remain at 180 bps, slightly narrower than the historical average.

The consensus is forecasting a reacceleration of FFO growth in 2025: The consensus estimate for 2025 FFO growth is 5.4%, which equates to an acceleration from 2024 growth of +230 bps. Applying the historical average negative estimate revision of -3% would arrive at a 2025 FFO growth rate of 3.8%, which would equate to a more modest acceleration from 2024 growth of +70 bps. With limited visibility into where interest rates may sit and where in the cycle the economy will land, we think a forecasted acceleration of this magnitude may be challenging to achieve. Still, we think certain stocks and subsectors can see growth accelerate in 2025 which should support valuations. (Link)

Figure 23: REIT subsector consensus 2024e FFO Growth
![](images/2f28ddf7c8c8ca1d4fd169279b63f9ef52ba2ce845f37f81d848602f85d2bfa9.jpg)
Source: FactSet, UBS

Figure 24: REIT subsector consensus 2025e FFO Growth
![](images/5d9856aa1631b618cbb8d6d8a6a102c167d85dbba508bd48689e5c57f4cf731b.jpg)
Source: FactSet, UBS

Investors appear to be looking past 2024 and valuing companies based on expected earnings acceleration in 2025. We regressed the change in consensus core FFO from 2024 to 2025 against the current NTM FFO multiple and found a correlation with an r-value of 76%. Importantly, this is a forward-looking effect that is specific to changing expectations from 2024 to 2025. We ran the same regression for the change in consensus core FFO from 2023 to 2024 and found a flatter correlation with an r-value of 35%. We think this indicates that the market is ascribing less value to 2024 earnings growth and looking ahead to anticipated growth in 2025.

## US REITs - Key Debates & Question Bank

In preparation for the upcoming conference season, we update our Key Debates & Question Bank for companies under coverage. As you prepare to meet with management teams, here is a list of key questions and topics that we would ask. (Link)

## U.S. Apartment REITs - 2024 SSRev to be driven by new lease rent growth & bad debt

To no surprise, the strong demand environment continued for Apartments in 4Q'23. This led to solid revenue growth for the Coastal REITs (AVB, EQR, ESS) of 3.8%. Though, supply pressure is starting to weigh on the the Sunbelt & mixed portfolios (CPT, MAA, UDR) with SSRev growth of 2.4%. For 2024, we expect these diverging trends to be sustained. Sitting a month and a half into 2024, there are many unknowns for the year with a range of macro outcomes. We think the REITs have taken an overall conservative approach to guidance with the key variance being new lease rent growth. (Link)

## U.S. Self Storage REITs - Spacing Out?

In our view, there were limited surprises in 4Q'23 earnings for the Storage REITs. In particular, operating metrics continued to moderate on soft demand and new customer price sensitivity. Though, the REITs indicated that elevated ECRIs remained the norm with no change in customer response. The continuation of these dynamics formulate to weigh on 2024. (Link)

## U.S. Single Family Rental REITs - Sleeping Well

In preparation for the upcoming conference season, we update our Key Debates & Question Bank for companies under coverage.

We dive into the moving pieces in 2024 guidance and lay out our argument for continuing to favor Coastal Apartment REITs.

We outline our thoughts on how Self Storage 4Q'23 earnings impact the 2024 outlook. We have modest expectations until evidence of improvement

We think the SFR results in 4Q'23 and guidance for 2024 suggest that both INVH & AMH are operating strong businesses. Both benefit from robust SFR demand drivers and favorable rental affordability relative to home ownership. This positions them well to sustain growth algorithms in 2024. Interestingly, both are finding their own path on slightly different operating strategies through the slower winter months. We think INVH's approach to maintain occupancy and AMH's continued focus on rate both have merit. For 2024, we think both companies are set up for a strong year. To this point, there does not appear to have been any change to the strong demand trends supporting the space. However, we think INVH should continue to outpace AMH in terms of SSRev growth. As it demonstrates it can achieve stronger growth, we think INVH can close the valuation gap to AMH.(Link)

We think the SFR results in 4Q'23 and guidance for 2024 suggest that both INVH & AMH are operating strong businesses with solid demand.

## U.S. Retail/Triple Net REITs - Big Boxes, Little Problems

We believe that Shopping Center REITs' 4Q'23 results provide evidence of the sustainability of the group's recent elevated growth algorithm. Across shopping center REITs in 4Q'23, average leasing spreads were robust at 13.1%. Notably, this was consistent with 3Q'23 levels and up year-over-year by 210 bps. Further, despite headwinds from tenant bankruptcies, sequential occupancy in the period improved by +60 bps to 94.7% (flat year-over-year). Together, we think these metrics reinforce the view that tenant demand has remained strong. Importantly, there is good visibility into continued strength in 2024 driving elevated leasing spreads for the Shopping Centers (UBSe avg 10+%) and occupancy gain (UBSe avg +30 bps yr/yr). We recognise there may be some quarter-to-quarter volatility from external factors, but overall we expect sustained positive trends that should reinforce the health of the group. (Link)

We believe that Shopping Center REITs' 4Q'23 results provide evidence of the sustainability of the group's recent elevated growth algorithm.

We think the outlook for Malls is less clear. SPG's core earnings algorithm continues to demonstrate positive momentum. This is apparent in its 4Q'23 domestic property NOI of 7.1% (3Q'23 of 4.2%) with an accelerating 2-yr stack of 13.1%. While the recent strength has been supported by expense savings that is unlikely to be sustainable, we think more modest NOI growth of 3-4% is achievable. Assuming that rental income can continue to grow at a similar level and rental income drove 60% of its NOI growth in 4Q'23 of 7%, it implies a sustainable run-rate of \~4%.

## Communications Infrastructure - Reiterating our Constructive View on Towers

Tower commentary was constructive during earnings, highlighting solid carrier activity in the US, with potential upside as we exit the year. Roughly 50%+ US towers have been upgraded with 5G equipment so far (UBSe 80% at TMUS, 50%+ at VZ, \~30% at AT&T) and we expect the full deployment to take another 2 years to complete. We believe the deployment cycle will gradually include network densification to support capacity needs, providing good visibility for MSD organic growth to continue. Meanwhile, higher interest costs are largely baked in the run-rate with NT maturities addressed and lowered floating mix (AMT at 11%, CCI 8%, SBAC 3%). We continue to believe the towers provide an attractive risk-reward given contractually locked-in demand & inflation/recession protection. SBAC is currently trading at just 15.5x '25E AFFO (vs. 22.1x 10-yr avg), AMT at 19.0x (vs. 21.3x), and CCI at 18.1x macro-only (vs. 22.3x), assuming 11x for fiber/small cells .(Link)

## Digital Realty Trust - Positive supply/demand trends but earnings growth limited in '24E

Tower commentary was constructive during earnings, highlighting solid carrier activity in the US, with potential upside as we exit the year.

Results and the 2024 outlook were generally in line on revenue/EBITDA but FFO/sh growth remains limited amid de-leveraging initiatives. With the recent ATM raise and \$1-1.5B of capital recycling, we now expect Core FFO/sh of \$6.63 in '24E (prior \$6.82; guide \$6.60-6.75) while our '25 estimate moves to \$6.89 (prior \$7.09). This represents 1%/4% growth vs. 3/4%previously. We believe the pricing outlook remains constructive and expect strong bookings performance in '24 but remain Neutral rated as we await increased visibility to accelerating FFO/sh growth. (Link)

## Equinix Inc - Delivering on AFFO/sh growth despite crosscurrents

Positive data points on pricing but de-leveraging initiatives to limit earnings growth in '24

Results were largely in line while the '24 outlook was lighter on rev growth but ahead on EBITDA/AFFO. For '24, mgmt expects CC revenues ex PPIs to grow 7-8% vs. prior UBSe

8.5% & long-term guidance of 8-10%. Mgmt called out longer sales cycles recently and higher churn (2.4% in 4Q vs. target for low end of 2-2.5% range) as customer budgets remain tight and optimizations continue. Capacity constraints are also limiting gross bookings in some key markets while efforts to refill churned space at higher rates results in a near-term vacancy drag. Despite the lower top-line outlook, guidance for EBITDA/ AFFO was 1-2% ahead of UBSe/Street given recent efficiency initiatives, lapping prior year investments and underlying operating leverage (EBITDA margins +150bps in '24 vs. stable last year ex power impact). We expect this to support AFFO/sh of \$35.10 (prior \$34.63), up 9.3% for the year vs. 8.7% in '23. (Link)

Focused on driving solid AFFO/sh growth as demand trends remain mixed

## CBRE Group Inc - 4Q23 Beat; FY24 Guidance Above Expectations

Relative to our forecast, CBRE reported higher revenues and higher adjusted expenses, leading to an adjusted EBITDA margin beat (14.2% vs 13.0% UBSe). On the top line, Advisory and REI performed better than expected, while GWS missed our expectations slightly. Core EBITDA was 14.5% higher than our estimates, driven by the Advisory segment. CBRE introduced guidance for FY24 core EPS of \$4.25-4.65, with the \$4.45 midpoint 1% higher than UBSe and 2.5% higher than current consensus of \$4.34. In FY24, EPS is expected to be heavily weighted to 2H24. The outlook for hitting record EPS in FY25 was softened. FY24 EPS guidance implies 11-21% y/y growth from FY23. (Link)

## LatAm REITs - Buy FUNO for exposure to Nearshoring, others are expensive.

As we updated our models, we maintain our Buy rating on Fibra Uno, Neutral ratings on Fibra Prologis and Vesta, and Sell rating on Fibra Danhos. We still believe the industrial sector will benefit from the nearshoring trend. However, we remain cautious on companies that are entirely exposed to this trend and have current valuations above the historical average, such as Fibra Prologis and Vesta. As we recently discussed, we are more positive on Fibra Uno ( raised our PT to P\$38 from P\$35), as the market does not seem to be pricing in its advisor internalization, while we believe the process is in good shape to materialize. On the other hand, we maintain our cautious outlook on the office sector and Fibra Danhos due to hybrid work arrangements and lasting changes resulting from the pandemic. (Link)

We maintain our Buy rating on Fibra Uno, Neutral ratings on Fibra Prologis and Vesta, and Sell rating on Fibra Danhos.

## Philippines Property Sector - Property call takeaways

We hosted Colliers International Philippines led by Managing Director Mr. Richard Raymundo and Director of Research Mr. Joey Bondoc, for a call on the Philippines' property outlook for the next 6-12 months. Overall, positive sentiment is skewed to malls and hotels (tourism-related), which are rebounding from pandemic lows. ## Economics and market strategy

UBS economists forecast Global GDP growth of 2.8% for 2024E and 3.2% for 2025E. Inflation expectations are at 5.8% for 2024E and 3.8% for 2025E. US 10Y bond yield forecasts are at 3.60% for 2024E and 3.75% for 2025E.

## Global Economics & Strategy - UBS World at a Glance

## Waiting for the data pendulum to swing back

The gap between US real GDP growth and the rest of the G7 over the last 6 months is at a 20-year high. The central thesis of most of our trading calls is that this can’t last, but the data so far disagrees. Given what is now priced, however, the risk reward over the next few months suggests taking a cautious view on equities, re-establishing received positions in fixed income, and being long volatility. When markets make new highs,

88% of the time they rise over the next 6 and 12m, but we’ve never seen markets make new highs when unemployment and intra-index volatility are this low.

The danger the next few weeks, however, is that we expect another hot US CPI print, which could perpetuate current price action. We likely need to get that print out of the way, and see weaker consumption/employment data, before many of our more defensive trades can work. But with that caveat, we like receiving the US front end, and in equity prefer defensive positioning through software, healthcare, transmission and distribution utilities and defence stocks. In FX, we like being long high-yielders such as NZD and CAD against CHF . In credit, we don’t see an immediate trigger for spread decompression so stick with carry trades (favouring HY/LL and in Europe Fins vs Nonfins). In EM, risk premia are too compressed; we largely stick with local market receivers in Brazil/Mexico/Korea, while being generally short EMFX (we identify 7 currencies). (Link)

## Key Messages

The gap between US real GDP growth and the rest of the G7 over the last 2 quarters is at a 20-year high (ex-pandemic). We expect it to close by Q2.

Global disinflation is intact but central banks are wary of easing too soon.

The divergence between US resilience and weakness elsewhere continues. In the 2nd half of 2023, GDP growth in the US averaged +4.1% annualized, while in the rest of the G7 it averaged -0.5% (simple average). Indeed, the UK and Japan were technically in recession while both France and Germany were a rounding error away from posting two negative quarters. Global growth fell from 3.9% in Q3 to 2.3% in Q4, and EM ex-China growth fell from 5.2% to 1.7%. Into year-end the impact of global monetary tightening was clearly still reverberating in the data.

However, there are some signs of a pick-up in global momentum in some of the Jan/Feb data. The global January manufacturing PMI data moved up over a full point and (at 49.7) is just a few ticks short of starting to expand—its highest level in 17 months. Most of that improvement comes from developed markets and it is showing up in forward looking components like new orders, even if some of the PMI strength is exaggerated by delivery delays due to the Red Sea attacks (PMI data interpret those as signs of demand strength; the delivery time component accounts for roughly a third of the European PMI improvement).

Global disinflation momentum remains intact but hit a roadblock in the US. Residual seasonality in January is a known problem, so we don’t take too much signal from the 40bp CPI print, but we are forecasting something similar for Feb (including for PCE) which does change the contour of what the FOMC will be looking at. That raises the risk that the 1st cut does not come in May but in June, but there is plenty of data before then to undo the ‘what’s the rush’ sentiment.

The pendulum of market pricing has swung back to validating a slow approach to central bank easing. We suspect that extrapolates too much from the US data (inflation in Europe came in below central banks forecasts, and wage and profit growth in Europe are slowing, for instance). For Japan we have scaled back our expectation of monetary tightening: we think NIRP ends in April but expect no hikes after that given the weakness in demand. (Link)

## US Economics - Our baseline still expects an economic slowdown.

\- CPI inflation and non-farm payrolls surprised on the upside in January, but retail sales fell, prior months were revised lower, and manufacturing production fell in January.

\- We expect real GDP expanded \~3.1% (Q4/Q4) in ’23. GDP then expands just 0.4% in '24E before reaccelerating to 2.4% in ‘25E.

\- The slower GDP growth should lead to a slower labor market and contraction that should raise the u-rate to 4.7% in ‘24E, helping to push core PCE inflation down to 1.9%, before rising to 2.1% in 2025E.

\- We expect the FOMC’s first cut at their May meeting with some risk of this slipping to June. With inflation back to target this year, we think the Fed will have considerable leeway to respond to weaker growth, returning the funds rate to 2.75 to 3% in 2024. (Link)

## Europe Economics - Germany at the core of Eurozone weakness

\- Following 0.5% in 2023, we forecast GDP growth of 0.6% for 2024 and 1.2% in 2025. We expect restrictive monetary policy, the weak external environment and fiscal consolidation to continue to exert headwinds to growth over the coming quarters. However, low unemployment and the recovery in real wage growth should support household consumption and prevent outright contraction. Following GDP growth - 0.1% q/q in Q3-23 and 0.0% q/q in Q4-23 – implying that the Eurozone narrowly avoided a technical recession in H2-23 - we forecast +0.2% q/q for Q1-24. However, the latest indicators (e.g. January/February PMIs) suggest downside risk for Q1-24. Germany is underperforming within the Eurozone - Q4 GDP fell 0.3% q/q. We see downside risks to our 2024 GDP growth forecast of 0.3%. Weak global demand, fiscal tightening and structural factors are weighing on growth.

\- Eurozone labor markets remain strong, with record-low unemployment and strong nominal wage growth.

\- We expect inflation to fall to the 2% target in 2025E. Despite good disinflation recently, we still see the risks as skewed to the upside.

\- ECB rates have peaked, at 4%. We expect the first cut in April 2024 (with the risk skewed towards June), with 100bps of cuts each in 2024 and 2025. (Link)

UK Economics - Technical recession in H2-23; indications of a pick-up at the start of Q1-24.

\- UK GDP is expected to grow 0.6% in 2024E and 1.5% in 2025E. Growth likely troughed in Q4-23 but is likely to be slow in early 2024 as the impact of BoE tightening weighs on consumers (mortgage debt service).

\- Inflation is now declining but the risk remains skewed to the upside. We expect the BoE to stay on hold until May 2024, when we expect the first rate cut.

\- We expect the fiscal policy stance to turn restrictive in 2024. However, the possible change in government next year introduces significant uncertainty around the fiscal path. (Link)

China Economics - Activity data remains mixed with consumer and manufacturing strength offset by continued property weakness

\- Travelling and tourism activities were strong during the 2024 CNY holiday, while property sales have still not bottomed.

\- January CPI deflation deepened. New credit recorded another historical high.

\- The PBC cut the 5yr LPR by 25bps in February, following the RRR cut and policy rate cuts of on-lending/re-discount facilities in January.

\- We see more rate cuts, an RRR cut, expansionary budgetary spending and further property policy easing ahead. (Link)

## Japan Economics - CPI inflation to slow but wage growth to accelerate.

\- Japan’s economy was in a technical recession in H2 last year and UBS expects a further contraction of real GDP in Q1. (Link)

\- However, we also expect a pickup in private domestic demand, consumption and capex with a rise in real wages and high profits from Q2, offsetting the moderation in external demand, including inbound tourists’ consumption.

\- CPI inflation is likely to slow with a fall in import prices but could then firm with higher wage growth.

\- The BoJ’s communication signaled the end of the current accommodative policy framework, especially negative interest rate policy and the 10yr yield target (YCC), to end soon (we expect April), while further rate hikes look unlikely through this year now.

## Australia & New Zealand - Although inflation is slowing it remains elevated and we think RBA and RBNZ cuts are backloaded to late '24

\- In Australia, Q3 real GDP showed quarterly momentum slowed to 0.2% q/q, but the y/y ticked up to 2.1%. We expect GDP to slow further to 1.5% y/y in 2024 (after 2.0%). However, inflation is seen remaining above the RBA’s target until 2025. Hence, we see the RBA on hold at 4.35%, with the first rate cut in Nov-24 of 25bps.

\- In NZ, real GDP in Q3-23 dropped -0.3% q/q and revisions revived the previous technical recession. UBS see real GDP growth still subdued at 1.3% y/y in 2024. We see headline CPI dropping to 2.2% y/y by Q4-24. For the RBNZ, we expect the cash rate to remain on hold at 5.50%; before the first 25bp rate cut in Nov-24. We judge the risk of another hike is low. (Link)

Asia ex China & Japan - Asia exports under pressure but with no visible outsized China effect; Korea exports improved

\- Semiconductor exports in Korea remained robust, suggesting the tech cycle improvement remains intact.

\- GDP growth results in 4Q23 were mixed in the region.

\- We expect 50/100/150bps of rate cuts in Thailand, the Philippines, and Indonesia in 2024.

## Regional allocation

Where to allocate capital? In this section we highlight our key indicators for regional allocation within global listed real estate.

## Regional tilts: quantitative model results

Below we summarise what our two quantitative models suggest for regional allocation. For the full report, please see Q-Series – How can investors capture alpha via regional real estate allocation? (3 September 2010).

Figure 35: Buy & Sell signals
![](images/0a3e7bed6a0cbe957387188fe251febdd04d29af3c08adb9411ef667d0e74626.jpg)
Source: UBS Proprietary Model. Buy/Sell signal is based on current pricing relative to its own history. Note: +/- 0.5 reflects weak buy and sell signals. +/- 1 represents strong buy and strong sell signals

Figure 36: Regional allocation model
![](images/e5247bca75a4d0d8a296bc8ab5ebaa34b19655d79fe43f136334947e158601b8.jpg)
Source: UBS Proprietary Model. Buy/Sell signal is based on each country's current relative pricing vs. the global average, and comparing that measure to its history: e.g. is it cheaper than normal? Note: +/- 0.5 reflects weak buy and sell signals. +/- 1 represents strong buy and strong sell signals. Raw regional tilts may not sum up to zero, as our calculation balances out the regional weights in the index weights.

## Valuation Method and Risk Statement

Key risks to global real estate include: (1) an increase in interest rates materially higher than our expectations could result in negative returns for real estate given the negative impact to real estate valuations; (2) asset allocation away from real estate toward higher growth sectors such as equities, if the return outlook for equities improves significantly toward double digit returns; (3) a prolonged economic downturn causes significant tenant bankruptcies; and (4) an oversupply of real estate emerges in terms of overbuilding in real estate markets, significantly increasing vacancies.

KEY DEFINITIONS: Forecast Stock Return (FSR) is defined as expected percentage price appreciation plus gross dividend yield over the next 12 months. In some cases, this yield may be based on accrued dividends. Market Return Assumption (MRA) is defined as the one-year local market interest rate plus 5% (a proxy for, and not a forecast of, the equity risk premium). Under Review (UR) Stocks may be flagged as UR by the analyst, indicating that the stock's price target and/or rating are subject to possible change in the near term, usually in response to an event that may affect the investment case or valuation. Short-Term Ratings reflect the expected near-term (up to three months) performance of the stock and do not reflect any change in the fundamental view or investment case. Equity Price Targets have an investment horizon of 12 months.

EXCEPTIONS AND SPECIAL CASES: UK and European Investment Fund ratings and definitions are: Buy: Positive on factors such as structure, management, performance record, discount; Neutral: Neutral on factors such as structure, management, performance record, discount; Sell: Negative on factors such as structure, management, performance record, discount. Core Banding Exceptions (CBE): Exceptions to the standard +/-6% bands may be granted by the Investment Review Committee (IRC). Factors considered by the IRC include the stock's volatility and the credit spread of the respective company's debt. As a result, stocks deemed to be very high or low risk may be subject to higher or lower bands as they relate to the rating. When such exceptions apply, they will be identified in the Company Disclosures table in the relevant research piece.

Associated Memberships: Japan Securities Dealers' Association, the Financial Futures Association of Japan, and Type II Financial Instruments Firms Association

UBS Securities Japan Co., Ltd. will receive a brokerage fee based on an individual contract and no standard upper limit or calculating method. For the trading of domestic stocks, consumption tax is added to the fee. For the trading of foreign stock, fee on the foreign stock exchange or foreign tax may be charged in addition to the domestic fee and tax. Those amounts may vary depending on the jurisdiction. There is a risk that a loss may occur due to a change in the price of the stock in the case of trading stocks, and that a loss may occur due to the exchange rate in the case of trading foreign stocks. There is a risk that a loss may occur due to a change in the price or performance of the properties in the portfolio in the case of trading REITs.

UBS Securities Japan Co., Ltd. will only receive the purchasing amounts for trading unlisted bonds (JGBs, municipals, government guaranteed bonds, corporate bonds) when UBS Securities Japan Co., Ltd. is the counterparty. There is a risk that a loss may occur due to a change in the price of the bond caused by the fluctuations in the interest rates, and that a loss may occur due to the exchange rate in the case of trading foreign bonds.

For the detailed information of commissions and risks associated with individual financial instrument transactions, please carefully read the documents provided prior to the execution of the contracts or other materials for customers, etc. before executing any transaction. If you need any clarification, please consult with our sales person in charge.

The Disclaimer relevant to Global Wealth Management clients follows the Global Disclaimer. The Disclaimer relevant to Credit Suisse Wealth Management follows the Global Wealth Management Disclaimer.

Investing in structured investments involves significant risks. For a detailed discussion of the risks involved in investing in any particular structured investment, you must read the relevant offering materials for that investment. Structured investments are unsecured obligations of a particular issuer with returns linked to the performance of an underlying asset. Depending on the terms of the investment, investors could lose all or a substantial portion of their investment based on the performance of the underlying asset. Investors could also lose their entire investment if the issuer becomes insolvent. UBS does not guarantee in any way the obligations or the financial condition of any issuer or the accuracy of any financial information provided by any issuer. Structured investments are not traditional investments and investing in a structured investment is not equivalent to investing directly in the underlying asset. Structured investments may have limited or no liquidity, and investors should be prepared to hold their investment to maturity. The return of structured investments may be limited by a maximum gain, participation rate or other feature. Structured investments may include call features and, if a structured investment is called early, investors would not earn any further return and may not be able to reinvest in similar investments with similar terms. Structured investments include costs and fees which are generally embedded in the price of the investment. The tax treatment of a structured investment may be complex and may differ from a direct investment in the underlying asset. UBS and its employees do not provide tax advice. Investors should consult their own tax advisor about their own tax situation before investing in any securities.

Important Information About Sustainable Investing Strategies: Sustainable investing strategies aim to consider and incorporate environmental, social and governance (ESG) factors into investment process and portfolio construction. Strategies across geographies approach ESG analysis and incorporate the findings in a variety of ways. Incorporating ESG factors or Sustainable Investing considerations may inhibit the portfolio manager’s ability to participate in certain investment opportunities that otherwise would be consistent with its investment objective and other principal investment strategies. The returns on a portfolio incorporating ESG factors or Sustainable Investing considerations may be lower or higher than portfolios where ESG factors, exclusions, or other sustainability issues are not considered by the portfolio manager, and the investment opportunities available to such portfolios may differ.
